On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"65b3927b-f4af-4ce7-aba8-75c53f640297","session_id":"1e5d5f9d-5afa-44b0-b008-6ae12e794d78","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_22adcf975f02490cbf4428db","is_error":false,"content":"# User account registration and authentication (JDBC)\n\nRegisters new user accounts into an SQL database and lets the stored credentials be replayed for\nlogin later. The caller supplies an already-open JDBC `Connection`; the class never opens, commits,\nrolls back or closes it.\n\n## Files\n\n| File | Purpose |\n| --- | --- |\n| `UserAccountService.java` | The implementation. Drop this single file into your project. |\n| `Demo.java` | Runnable end-to-end demonstration against any JDBC URL. |\n| `UserAccountServiceTest.java` | Functional verification against a real SQL engine (H2). 132 checks. |\n| `UserAccountServiceJdbcContractTest.java` | Verification with hand-rolled JDBC doubles that assert the exact SQL text and bound parameters. 77 checks. |\n| `README.md` | This document. |\n\nJava 17 or newer, no dependencies beyond the JDK (`Demo` and the engine test need a JDBC driver at\nruntime; see [Compile and run](#compile-and-run)).\n\n## API\n\n```java\nUserAccountService service = new UserAccountService();      // or (algorithm, iterations, saltBytes, hashBytes)\n\nservice.ensureSchema(connection);                           // optional: CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS\nservice.registerUser(connection, \"alice\", \"a passphrase\");  // -> Account, or throws\nservice.authenticate(connection, \"alice\", \"a passphrase\");  // -> boolean\nservice.deleteUser(connection, \"alice\");                    // -> boolean\nservice.credentialsNeedUpgrade(storedIterations);           // -> boolean, migrate flag\nservice.deriveMillis();                                     // -> long, for tuning the work factor\n```\n\nBoth `registerUser` and `authenticate` also take `char[]` passwords, which is the better form: a\n`char[]` can be wiped afterwards, a `String` cannot.\n\nChecked exceptions:\n\n* `UserAccountService.DuplicateUsernameException` -- the username is taken.\n* `UserAccountService.InvalidValueException` (an `IllegalArgumentException`) -- the username or\n  password breaks the policy below.\n* `SQLException` -- the database failed.\n\n### Policy\n\n* Username: 3-64 characters of `[A-Za-z0-9._-]`, first character a letter or digit. Anything else\n  (`'`, `;`, `--`, spaces, newlines, Unicode) is rejected at the boundary.\n* Password: 10-1024 characters, no control characters. No other syntax rule -- passphrases with\n  spaces and punctuation are fine, and `registerUser` never rejects a *password* for looking like\n  SQL.\n\n## What is stored\n\n`ensureSchema` creates:\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS app_user (\n    username      VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,\n    algorithm     VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,\n    iterations    INT           NOT NULL,\n    salt          VARBINARY(128) NOT NULL,\n    password_hash VARBINARY(128) NOT NULL,\n    created_at    TIMESTAMP     NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,\n    CONSTRAINT pk_app_user PRIMARY KEY (username)\n)\n```\n\n`VARCHAR`/`VARBINARY`/`INT`/`TIMESTAMP` are accepted as written by H2, PostgreSQL, MySQL/MariaDB and\nSQL Server. On Oracle use `RAW(n)` and on SQLite `BLOB(n)` instead of `VARBINARY(n)`; the rest of\nthe code is unchanged. Skip `ensureSchema` and bring your own DDL if you prefer -- the service only\nneeds a table shaped like the one above.\n\nThe password itself is never stored, never logged and never appears in any bound parameter or\nstatement text (`UserAccountServiceJdbcContractTest` asserts both).\n\n## Design decisions\n\n**Passwords are salted, stretched derived keys, not digests.** Each registration draws 16 fresh\nrandom bytes from `SecureRandom` and derives a 32-byte key with `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` at 310 000\nrounds (the OWASP 2023 figure for that construction). Same password, different salt, different\nstored bytes -- so a leaked table cannot be rainbow-tabled and two users with the same password are\nindistinguishable. `MessageDigest.isEqual` (constant time, and length-safe) does the comparison.\n\n**The algorithm and iteration count are stored per row.** Raising `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS` later does not\nlock anyone out: verification honours the row's own parameters, so existing accounts keep working\nafter the work factor changes. `credentialsNeedUpgrade(storedIterations)` is the detection hook --\ncall it with the iteration count you read from the row and, when it says yes, migrate that account.\nThis class deliberately stops at *detection*: it has no write path for an existing row, so the\nre-hash (which needs the plaintext, so only ever during a successful login) is yours to place behind\nwhatever review and audit trail a credential change needs. Set the work factor correctly at launch\nrather than planning to migrate later.\n\n**Every statement is a `PreparedStatement` with bound parameters.** The lookup is exactly\n`WHERE username = ?`. Not `LIKE`, not `UPPER(...) = UPPER(?)`, not string concatenation -- the first\ntwo are the \"it looks parameterised\" rewrites that silently match several accounts, and the engine\ntest and the contract test each reject them (see [Tests](#tests)).\n\n**Duplicates are settled by the database, not by a prior SELECT.** A check-then-insert races: two\nconcurrent requests both see \"free\" and both insert. Instead there is one `INSERT`, and a\nunique-constraint violation (`SQLIntegrityConstraintViolationException`, or any SQLState class 23\nfor drivers that report a bare `SQLException`) becomes `DuplicateUsernameException`. Two real\nconnections racing on one username leave exactly one row -- that is a test.\n\n**The caller's transaction stays usable after a rejected registration.** When auto-commit is off, the\n`INSERT` runs inside a savepoint that is rolled back on failure. On PostgreSQL a failed statement\notherwise aborts the whole transaction, and every later statement on that connection would fail.\n\n**The service never touches transaction state.** No `commit`, `rollback`, `close` or\n`setAutoCommit` on your connection -- asserted against doubles that count every call.\n\n**Login does not disclose which accounts exist.** A lookup that finds no row still spends one full\nkey derivation on a dummy credential before returning `false`. The dummy is produced by running the\nreal KDF at construction time, not by a hand-written constant. That distinction matters: a constant\nthat a decoder rejects makes the branch return early, so the account-existence oracle comes back\nwith no error and no failing assertion, only a timing difference. Measured with the test work factor:\nunknown user 73 ms, existing user 73 ms (and 288 ms vs 282 ms at the contract suite's setting).\n\n**Rows are validated before any work is spent on them.** A tampered row claiming `iterations = 1`,\n`iterations = 999999999`, an empty salt, a truncated key or `algorithm = 'MD5'` is refused outright\nrather than fed to `PBEKeySpec`, which accepts all of those. Refusal also costs a dummy derivation,\nso it is not distinguishable from a wrong password either.\n\nThe bounds check is load-bearing in a way that is easy to test *wrong*: if it is missing, a row\nclaiming 999 999 999 iterations still fails to authenticate (the derived key does not match), it\njust takes half an hour of CPU to find that out. Asserting only the boolean return value would pass.\nBoth suites therefore assert that the refusal happens inside a 5 s budget, on a worker thread.\n\n### Known trade-offs\n\n* Timing equalisation covers *response time*, not CPU load: a burst of probes against existing\n  accounts still shows up in server metrics. Rate limiting belongs in front of this.\n* `SecureRandom` blocks only if the platform entropy source is starved; on a headless container\n  consider `-Djava.security.egd=file:/dev/urandom`.\n* PBKDF2 is a sound choice, and memory-hard functions (Argon2id) are stronger if you can add a\n  dependency. The `algorithm` column exists precisely so that swap is a code change, not a migration.\n* Passwords are checked for length and control characters, not against a breach list. That is an\n  integration point for you, not something to ship hardcoded.\n\n## Compile and run\n\nNothing to build beyond `javac`. To run `Demo` and the engine test you need a JDBC driver; H2 is a\nsingle jar:\n\n```bash\ncurl -sSLo h2.jar https://repo1.maven.org/maven2/com/h2database/h2/2.2.224/h2-2.2.224.jar\n\njavac -cp h2.jar *.java                    # compiles all four sources\njava  -cp h2.jar:. Demo                    # in-memory database\njava  -cp h2.jar:. Demo \"jdbc:h2:./accounts\"   # or any URL whose driver is on the path\n```\n\n`Demo` output on this machine:\n\n```\nJDBC url : jdbc:h2:mem:demo;DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1\none key derivation takes 579 ms at 310000 PBKDF2 rounds\n\nregistered   Account[alice, PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256, iterations=310000]\nregistered   Account[b.o-brien, PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256, iterations=310000]\nregistered   Account[chen, PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256, iterations=310000]\n--- what the database actually holds -----------------------------\nalice        PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256      310000 rounds  salt=f1634e58f0233515...  hash=71ebc8c81fbc2afc...\nb.o-brien    PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256      310000 rounds  salt=b05e45db31750fb0...  hash=c065649a1b5d746a...\nchen         PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256      310000 rounds  salt=a9508885d91e3c0e...  hash=712683973f585725...\n  (no column holds the password, and no two rows share a salt)\n\n--- authenticating -------------------------------------------\nACCEPTED     'alice' with 'correct horse battery staple'\nREJECTED     'alice' with 'Correct horse battery staple'\nACCEPTED     'b.o-brien' with 'trap' or 1=1 --oracle quote'\nREJECTED     'b.o-brien' with 'trap' or 1=1 --o'\nREJECTED     'nobody' with 'whatever password'\nREJECTED     'alic_' with 'correct horse battery staple'\nREJECTED     'Alice' with 'correct horse battery staple'\n--- duplicate handling ---------------------------------------\nrefused      username is already registered: alice\n--- policy enforcement ---------------------------------------\nrefused      username must be 3-64 characters of [A-Za-z0-9._-] and start with a letter or digit\nrefused      password must be 10-1024 characters long\n--- the caller owns the transaction --------------------------\nrolled back  the registration of 'rolled-back'\nREJECTED     'rolled-back' with 'a long enough passphrase'\n```\n\n`alic_` would match `alice` if the lookup used `LIKE`, and `Alice` would match if it case-folded;\nboth are rejected because the predicate is a plain `username = ?`. The `579 ms` figure is what one\nderivation costs on an otherwise idle 4-core box; the same call measured 1177 ms while the mutation\nbattery below was saturating all four cores, so treat it as a tuning input rather than a spec.\n\n### Tests\n\n```bash\njava -cp h2.jar:. UserAccountServiceTest             # 132 checks, ~12 s, needs h2.jar\njava      UserAccountServiceJdbcContractTest         #  77 checks, ~22 s, no driver needed\n```\n\nBoth print `OK <n> checks passed` and exit 0, or list the failing checks and exit 1.\n\n`UserAccountServiceTest` runs against a real engine: registration and login round-trip, distinct\nsalts, the stored key equals an independently computed PBKDF2, injection payloads as passwords,\ncase-sensitivity and wildcard non-matching, policy boundaries, nine kinds of tampered row (each of\nwhich must be refused *promptly*, not after deriving it), salt freshness when the same username is\nre-registered, a row stored at an older work factor, two connections racing on one username, and\ntransaction semantics on a file-backed database (rollback visibility, surviving the failed insert,\nthe service not committing for you).\n\n`UserAccountServiceJdbcContractTest` runs against `java.lang.reflect.Proxy` doubles with no driver\ninvolved, and asserts the *contract* an engine cannot: the exact prepared statement text, that the\n`INSERT` has five placeholders with the right value in each slot, that no statement text ever\ncontains user input, that a query returning two rows is refused instead of the first row being\ntrusted, that a well-formed row really does authenticate (so a build that rejects everything cannot\npass), that the salt is fresh entropy on every registration, that a corrupt row is refused *within a\ntime budget* rather than derived, and that\n`commit`/`rollback`/`close`/`setAutoCommit` are never called.\n\nThe two suites are complementary, not redundant. A `Proxy` double replays scripted rows, so it\ncannot see a predicate that matches too much (`LIKE` survives it, H2 does not); a live engine\naccepts only rows it can represent, so it cannot hand the code an iteration-count bomb or a 4-byte\nkey (the double can). Several mutants are killed by exactly one of the two, which is why both run.\n\n### Mutation testing\n\nThe suites were checked by injecting 18 defects into `UserAccountService.java` one at a time,\nrebuilding, and confirming the tests fail. All 18 were detected; the right-hand column names the\nsuite(s) that caught it (`H2` = engine suite, `C` = contract suite):\n\n| Injected defect | Caught by |\n| --- | --- |\n| Lookup predicate rewritten to `username LIKE ?` | H2 + C |\n| Lookup case-folded: `UPPER(username) = UPPER(?)` | H2 + C |\n| Username concatenated into the lookup SQL | H2 + C |\n| Password stored verbatim instead of the derived key | H2 + C |\n| Constant (non-random) salt | H2 + C |\n| Salt derived from the username instead of the RNG | H2 + C |\n| Dummy derivation removed from the unknown-user path | H2 + C |\n| `==` instead of `MessageDigest.isEqual` | H2 + C |\n| Verification always returns false | H2 + C |\n| Stored `iterations` ignored, service default used | H2 + C |\n| Duplicate error swallowed, reported as success | H2 + C |\n| Any `SQLException` classified as a duplicate username | H2 + C |\n| Service commits the caller's transaction | H2 + C |\n| Savepoint around the guarded insert removed | C |\n| Stored iteration bounds check removed | H2 + C |\n| Empty stored salt accepted | C |\n| Derived key truncated to 8 bytes | H2 |\n| Default work factor weakened to 1 000 rounds | H2 |\n\nThree of these are worth calling out, because each one taught me something about the tests rather\nthan about the code:\n\n* **Salt derived from the username** was *not* caught by the original suite. It passes \"two accounts\n  have different salts\", it verifies correctly, and the stored key still equals an independently\n  computed PBKDF2. The check that kills it registers the *same* username twice and compares salts.\n* **`==` instead of `MessageDigest.isEqual`**, and **verification always returning false**, were only\n  caught once a positive-path check existed. Every other contract assertion expects a rejection, so a\n  build that rejects everything looked healthy.\n* **The savepoint removal is caught only by the contract suite**, and that is inherent rather than a\n  gap I can close: savepoints matter because PostgreSQL aborts a transaction after a failed statement,\n  which H2 does not do. No observable H2 behaviour changes, so only a JDBC-level assertion that a\n  savepoint was opened and rolled back can see it. Likewise the 8-byte key and the weakened work\n  factor are properties of the shipped defaults, which the contract suite deliberately overrides to\n  run fast, so only the engine suite pins them.\n\n## Using it in an application\n\n```java\n// Once, from your migration/startup path:\nUserAccountService service = new UserAccountService();\n\n// Per request, with the connection your pool or ORM already opened:\npublic boolean login(Connection connection, String username, char[] password) throws SQLException {\n    return service.authenticate(connection, username, password);\n}\n\npublic void signup(Connection connection, String username, char[] password) throws SQLException {\n    boolean previous = connection.getAutoCommit();\n    connection.setAutoCommit(false);\n    try {\n        service.registerUser(connection, username, password);\n        connection.commit();                       // you decide when the account becomes real\n    } catch (UserAccountService.DuplicateUsernameException taken) {\n        connection.rollback();\n        throw new UsernameAlreadyUsedException();  // render a form error, do not confirm existence\n    } finally {\n        connection.setAutoCommit(previous);\n    }\n}\n```\n\nNotes for production:\n\n* Tune `iterations` so one derivation costs roughly 100-500 ms on *your* hardware\n  (`service.deriveMillis()`); 310 000 measures ~580 ms on this machine and will be faster on newer\n  CPUs.\n* `authenticate` returning `false` and `DuplicateUsernameException` both reveal account existence to\n  whoever triggered them. Return a generic failure to end users, and rate-limit.\n* One connection per thread. `Connection` is not thread-safe; this class is.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"e6bd54bc-e356-45ca-9dff-7999225595f8","session_id":"1e5d5f9d-5afa-44b0-b008-6ae12e794d78","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"e6bd54bc-e356-45ca-9dff-7999225595f8","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now let me read all source files. database and authenticates them again later.\n *\n * <p>The caller owns the {@link Connection}: this class never commits, rolls back or closes it.\n * Every statement is a {@link PreparedStatement} with bound parameters, so user input is never\n * spliced into SQL text.\n *\n * <p>Passwords are never stored. Each account gets a fresh random salt and a PBKDF2 derived key\n * (salt and key stored as SQL binary columns, algorithm and iteration count stored alongside so\n * the work factor can be raised later without invalidating old rows). Verification re-derives the\n * key from the supplied password and the stored salt and compares the two in constant time.\n *\n * <p>Instances are immutable and safe for concurrent use; {@link Connection} itself is not, so a\n * connection should not be shared between threads without external synchronisation.\n */\npublic final class UserAccountService {\n\n    /** Key-derivation function recommended for new deployments. */\n    public static final String DEFAULT_ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n\n    /**\n     * PBKDF2 rounds for new accounts. Chosen to match the OWASP 2023 guidance for\n     * PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 (310 000 iterations); raise it if your hardware is faster than the\n     * reference measurement reported by {@link #deriveMillis()}.\n     */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 310_000;\n\n    /** Bytes of fresh randomness per account. 128 bits is far beyond any collision concern. */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_SALT_BYTES = 16;\n\n    /** Bytes of derived key stored per account (256 bits, the full PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 output). */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_HASH_BYTES = 32;\n\n    /** Rejected above this: a corrupted row must not be able to make us spin for minutes. */\n    private static final int MAX_STORED_ITERATIONS = 20_000_000;\n\n    /** Rejected below this: a corrupted row must not quietly downgrade every account to no work. */\n    private static final int MIN_STORED_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n\n    private static final String TABLE = \"app_user\";\n\n    private static final String CREATE_TABLE =\n            \"CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS \" + TABLE + \" (\"\n            + \" username VARCHAR(64) NOT NULL,\"\n            + \" algorithm VARCHAR(64) NOT NULL,\"\n            + \" iterations INT NOT NULL,\"\n            + \" salt VARBINARY(128) NOT NULL,\"\n            + \" password_hash VARBINARY(128) NOT NULL,\"\n            + \" created_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,\"\n            + \" CONSTRAINT pk_\" + TABLE + \" PRIMARY KEY (username)\"\n            + \")\";\n\n    private static final String INSERT_USER =\n            \"INSERT INTO \" + TABLE\n            + \" (username, algorithm, iterations, salt, password_hash) VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?)\";\n\n    // The predicate is an exact equality on a bound parameter: no LIKE, no case folding, no\n    // concatenation, so neither SQL injection nor a widened match is possible.\n    private static final String SELECT_USER =\n            \"SELECT algorithm, iterations, salt, password_hash FROM \" + TABLE\n            + \" WHERE username = ?\";\n\n    private static final String DELETE_USER = \"DELETE FROM \" + TABLE + \" WHERE username = ?\";\n\n    private static final String DROP_TABLE = \"DROP TABLE IF EXISTS \" + TABLE;\n\n    private static final Pattern USERNAME_PATTERN = Pattern.compile(\"[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]{2,63}\");\n\n    private static final int MIN_PASSWORD_CHARS = 10;\n    private static final int MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS = 1024;\n\n    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n    private static final AtomicLong SAVEPOINT_SEQ = new AtomicLong();\n\n    private final String algorithm;\n    private final int iterations;\n    private final int saltBytes;\n    private final int hashBytes;\n\n    /**\n     * Derived key for a password that no account can ever have, plus that password. Used to spend\n     * the same key-derivation time on an unknown username as on a known one, so response time does\n     * not disclose which accounts exist. Built lazily by running the real KDF, never hand-written.\n     */\n    private volatile byte[] dummyKey;\n    private volatile char[] dummyPassword;\n\n    /** An instance using the recommended parameters. */\n    public UserAccountService() {\n        this(DEFAULT_ALGORITHM, DEFAULT_ITERATIONS, DEFAULT_SALT_BYTES, DEFAULT_HASH_BYTES);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * An instance with an explicit work factor, for deployments that need to tune the\n     * cost/latency trade-off or for tests that cannot afford 300 000 rounds per call.\n     *\n     * @throws IllegalArgumentException if the parameters are unusable\n     */\n    public UserAccountService(String algorithm, int iterations, int saltBytes, int hashBytes) {\n        if (algorithm == null || !algorithm.startsWith(\"PBKDF2\")) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"algorithm must be a PBKDF2 variant, got: \" + algorithm);\n        }\n        if (iterations < MIN_STORED_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_STORED_ITERATIONS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"iterations must be in [\" + MIN_STORED_ITERATIONS + \", \" + MAX_STORED_ITERATIONS + \"]\");\n        }\n        if (saltBytes < 8 || saltBytes > 64) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"saltBytes must be in [8, 64]\");\n        }\n        if (hashBytes < 16 || hashBytes > 64) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"hashBytes must be in [16, 64]\");\n        }\n        try {\n            SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(algorithm);\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"algorithm not available on this JVM: \" + algorithm, e);\n        }\n        this.algorithm = algorithm;\n        this.iterations = iterations;\n        this.saltBytes = saltBytes;\n        this.hashBytes = hashBytes;\n    }\n\n    /** The recommended number of PBKDF2 rounds for accounts created by this instance. */\n    public int iterations() {\n        return iterations;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Creates the account table if it is not already there. The types used are\n     * {@code VARCHAR}/{@code VARBINARY}/{@code INT}/{@code TIMESTAMP}, which H2, PostgreSQL,\n     * MySQL/MariaDB and SQL Server accept as written; on Oracle or SQLite replace\n     * {@code VARBINARY(n)} with {@code RAW(n)} / {@code BLOB(n)} before calling this.\n     *\n     * <p>Primary-key-on-username is what makes registration race-safe: two concurrent inserts of\n     * the same username cannot both succeed, and the loser gets\n     * {@link DuplicateUsernameException} rather than a silently duplicated row.\n     */\n    public void ensureSchema(Connection connection) throws SQLException {\n        try (Statement st = connection.createStatement()) {\n            st.execute(CREATE_TABLE);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Removes the account table. Intended for tests and demos. */\n    public void dropSchema(Connection connection) throws SQLException {\n        try (Statement st = connection.createStatement()) {\n            st.execute(DROP_TABLE);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Creates an account for {@code username} with {@code password}.\n     *\n     * <p>The caller's transaction (if any) stays usable when the username is already taken: the\n     * insert runs inside a savepoint that is rolled back on failure, which matters on engines such\n     * as PostgreSQL where a failed statement otherwise aborts the whole transaction.\n     *\n     * @param password validated but not cleared; wipe the caller's array if it matters to you\n     * @throws InvalidValueException the username or password breaks the documented policy\n     * @throws DuplicateUsernameException {@code username} is already registered\n     * @throws SQLException from the database\n     */\n    public Account registerUser(Connection connection, String username, char[] password)\n            throws SQLException, DuplicateUsernameException {\n        validateUsername(username);\n        validatePassword(password);\n\n        byte[] salt = new byte[saltBytes];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(salt);\n        byte[] hash = derive(password, salt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n\n        // A single INSERT is the whole write, so there is no partially registered account to clean\n        // up, and \"check then insert\" (which would let two racing requests both pass the check) is\n        // replaced by \"insert and read back the unique-constraint violation\".\n        Savepoint savepoint = null;\n        if (!connection.getAutoCommit()) {\n            savepoint = connection.setSavepoint(\"REG_\" + SAVEPOINT_SEQ.incrementAndGet());\n        }\n        try (PreparedStatement insert = connection.prepareStatement(INSERT_USER)) {\n            insert.setString(1, username);\n            insert.setString(2, algorithm);\n            insert.setInt(3, iterations);\n            insert.setBytes(4, salt);\n            insert.setBytes(5, hash);\n            insert.executeUpdate();\n        } catch (SQLException e) {\n            if (savepoint != null) {\n                connection.rollback(savepoint);\n            }\n            if (isUniqueConstraintViolation(e)) {\n                throw new DuplicateUsernameException(username);\n            }\n            throw e;\n        }\n        return new Account(username, algorithm, iterations);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Convenience overload for callers that already hold a {@link String}. A {@code String} cannot\n     * be wiped from memory afterwards, so prefer the {@code char[]} form when the password came\n     * from a console or a request body you control.\n     */\n    public Account registerUser(Connection connection, String username, String password)\n            throws SQLException, DuplicateUsernameException {\n        if (password == null) {\n            throw new InvalidValueException(\"password must not be null\");\n        }\n        return registerUser(connection, username, password.toCharArray());\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Checks {@code username} / {@code password} against the stored credential.\n     *\n     * @return {@code true} only if the account exists and the password re-derives to the stored key\n     * @throws SQLException from the database\n     */\n    public boolean authenticate(Connection connection, String username, char[] password)\n            throws SQLException {\n        // Reject malformed input before touching the database, but do not reveal which half was bad\n        // and do not let an empty username skip the constant-work path below.\n        if (username == null || password == null || username.isEmpty() || password.length == 0) {\n            burnKeyDerivation();\n            return false;\n        }\n        StoredCredential stored;\n        try (PreparedStatement select = connection.prepareStatement(SELECT_USER)) {\n            select.setString(1, username);\n            try (ResultSet rs = select.executeQuery()) {\n                if (!rs.next()) {\n                    // Same cost as a real verification: an attacker timing logins cannot tell an\n                    // existing account from a missing one.\n                    burnKeyDerivation();\n                    return false;\n                }\n                stored = readCredential(rs);\n            }\n        }\n        if (stored == null) {\n            // Row is corrupt or names a KDF this JVM cannot run: refuse it, and spend the normal\n            // amount of time so the failure is not distinguishable from a wrong password.\n            burnKeyDerivation();\n            return false;\n        }\n        byte[] candidate = derive(password, stored.salt, stored.iterations, stored.algorithm,\n                stored.hash.length);\n        // Length-independent constant-time comparison over fixed-size keys.\n        return MessageDigest.isEqual(stored.hash, candidate);\n    }\n\n    /** Convenience {@link String} overload of {@link #authenticate(Connection, String, char[])}. */\n    public boolean authenticate(Connection connection, String username, String password)\n            throws SQLException {\n        return password != null && authenticate(connection, username, password.toCharArray());\n    }\n\n    /** Deletes an account. Returns {@code true} if a row was removed. */\n    public boolean deleteUser(Connection connection, String username) throws SQLException {\n        try (PreparedStatement delete = connection.prepareStatement(DELETE_USER)) {\n            delete.setString(1, username);\n            return delete.executeUpdate() == 1;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Whether stored credentials created by an older, cheaper work factor should be re-derived at\n     * the current {@link #iterations()}. Call with the iteration count read back from a row; the\n     * usual place to act on {@code true} is a successful login, when the plaintext password is\n     * available anyway. This is detection only: the class has no write path for an existing row, so\n     * persisting the upgraded credential is the caller's operation.\n     */\n    public boolean credentialsNeedUpgrade(int storedIterations) {\n        return storedIterations < iterations;\n    }\n\n    /** Milliseconds for one key derivation with this instance's parameters; for tuning. */\n    public long deriveMillis() {\n        char[] probe = \"tuning-only-passphrase-0123456789\".toCharArray();\n        byte[] salt = new byte[saltBytes];\n        long start = System.nanoTime();\n        derive(probe, salt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n        return (System.nanoTime() - start) / 1_000_000L;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Validates the bits read back from a row before spending any work on them: a tampered or\n     * truncated row must be refused, not fed to {@link PBEKeySpec} (which happily accepts an empty\n     * salt) and not allowed to select an unbounded iteration count.\n     */\n    private StoredCredential readCredential(ResultSet rs) throws SQLException {\n        String storedAlgorithm = rs.getString(1);\n        int storedIterations = rs.getInt(2);\n        byte[] salt = rs.getBytes(3);\n        byte[] hash = rs.getBytes(4);\n        if (storedAlgorithm == null || !storedAlgorithm.startsWith(\"PBKDF2\")) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (storedIterations < MIN_STORED_ITERATIONS || storedIterations > MAX_STORED_ITERATIONS) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (salt == null || salt.length < 8 || salt.length > 128) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (hash == null || hash.length < 16 || hash.length > 128) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        return new StoredCredential(storedAlgorithm, storedIterations, salt, hash);\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isUniqueConstraintViolation(SQLException e) {\n        if (e instanceof SQLIntegrityConstraintViolationException) {\n            return true;\n        }\n        // Some drivers report a unique violation as a bare SQLException, so SQLState has to be\n        // enough on its own. Class 23 is integrity constraint violation: MySQL/SQL Server 23000,\n        // PostgreSQL 23505, DB2 23505, H2 23505 (measured in every H2 compatibility mode).\n        String sqlState = e.getSQLState();\n        return sqlState != null && sqlState.startsWith(\"23\");\n    }\n\n    private static void validateUsername(String username) {\n        if (username == null || !USERNAME_PATTERN.matcher(username).matches()) {\n            throw new InvalidValueException(\n                    \"username must be 3-64 characters of [A-Za-z0-9._-] and start with a letter or digit\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static void validatePassword(char[] password) {\n        if (password == null) {\n            throw new InvalidValueException(\"password must not be null\");\n        }\n        if (password.length < MIN_PASSWORD_CHARS || password.length > MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS) {\n            throw new InvalidValueException(\"password must be \" + MIN_PASSWORD_CHARS + \"-\"\n                    + MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" characters long\");\n        }\n        for (char c : password) {\n            if (c < 0x20 || c == 0x7F) {\n                throw new InvalidValueException(\"password must not contain control characters\");\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations, String algorithm,\n            int hashBytes) {\n        PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, hashBytes * 8);\n        try {\n            SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(algorithm);\n            return factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            // Only reachable if the row names a KDF this JVM does not ship, which readCredential\n            // already screens for; a wrong answer here must not look like \"wrong password\".\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"cannot derive key with \" + algorithm, e);\n        } finally {\n            spec.clearPassword();\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Runs one key derivation that can never authenticate anything, so that a lookup that found no\n     * row costs the same as one that did.\n     */\n    private void burnKeyDerivation() {\n        byte[] key = dummyKey;\n        char[] password = dummyPassword;\n        if (key == null || password == null) {\n            synchronized (this) {\n                if (dummyKey == null) {\n                    char[] probe = randomPassword();\n                    byte[] probeSalt = new byte[saltBytes];\n                    RANDOM.nextBytes(probeSalt);\n                    // Real KDF over a password that exists in no table, so the dummy digest is\n                    // structurally identical to a stored one instead of a hand-written constant\n                    // that a decoder might reject and silently skip the work.\n                    dummyKey = derive(probe, probeSalt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n                    dummyPassword = probe;\n                }\n                key = dummyKey;\n                password = dummyPassword;\n            }\n        }\n        // A salt that belongs to no stored account, so the derivation below can never be the basis\n        // of a successful login and can never collide with the recorded digest.\n        byte[] throwawaySalt = new byte[saltBytes];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(throwawaySalt);\n        byte[] probe = derive(password, throwawaySalt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n        if (MessageDigest.isEqual(probe, key)) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"internal error: dummy credential collision\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static char[] randomPassword() {\n        char[] buffer = new char[32];\n        for (int i = 0; i < buffer.length; i++) {\n            buffer[i] = (char) ('!' + RANDOM.nextInt(93));\n        }\n        return buffer;\n    }\n\n    /** Public account information about a registered user. Never carries credential material. */\n    public static final class Account {\n\n        private final String username;\n        private final String algorithm;\n        private final int iterations;\n\n        Account(String username, String algorithm, int iterations) {\n            this.username = username;\n            this.algorithm = algorithm;\n            this.iterations = iterations;\n        }\n\n        public String username() {\n            return username;\n        }\n\n        public String algorithm() {\n            return algorithm;\n        }\n\n        public int iterations() {\n            return iterations;\n        }\n\n        @Override\n        public String toString() {\n            return \"Account[\" + username + \", \" + algorithm + \", iterations=\" + iterations + \"]\";\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** A row read back from the account table. Package-private; never handed out. */\n    private static final class StoredCredential {\n\n        private final String algorithm;\n        private final int iterations;\n        private final byte[] salt;\n        private final byte[] hash;\n\n        StoredCredential(String algorithm, int iterations, byte[] salt, byte[] hash) {\n            this.algorithm = algorithm;\n            this.iterations = iterations;\n            this.salt = salt;\n            this.hash = hash;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Raised when a username or password violates the policy documented on the methods. */\n    public static final class InvalidValueException extends IllegalArgumentException {\n\n        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n\n        InvalidValueException(String message) {\n            super(message);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Raised when an account with the requested username already exists. */\n    public static final class DuplicateUsernameException extends Exception {\n\n        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n\n        DuplicateUsernameException(String username) {\n            // Deliberately does not say whether the account exists for anyone other than the\n            // registered owner; the username is echoed back to the requester, who supplied it.\n            super(\"username is already registered: \" + username);\n        }\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"a4a4317d-8026-4024-8f0f-5698b9af60da","session_id":"1e5d5f9d-5afa-44b0-b008-6ae12e794d78","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_4c5ccfa6b6ae4cf584ce3132","is_error":false,"content":"import java.sql.Connection;\nimport java.sql.DriverManager;\nimport java.sql.ResultSet;\nimport java.sql.SQLException;\nimport java.sql.Statement;\n\n/**\n * Runnable end-to-end demo of {@link UserAccountService}: registers accounts, authenticates them,\n * and shows what is actually stored. name: 'Account Takeover: Exposed API Key'\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n  - id: F1004\n    name: Access with Stolen Session Cookie\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n---\n# Performing Cryptographic Audit of Application\n\n## Overview\n\nA cryptographic audit systematically reviews an application's use of cryptographic primitives, protocols, and key management to identify vulnerabilities such as weak algorithms, insecure modes, hardcoded keys, insufficient entropy, and protocol misconfigurations. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 Not a finding.\n\n2. **Lazy init race in `burnKeyDerivation()`** — `dummyKey`/`dummyPassword` are separate volatile fields; double-checked-locking on `dummyKey == null` inside `synchronized(this)`. The check reads the *local* `key`/`password` captured before the sync block, then inside sync re-checks `dummyKey == null`. Consider thread A: reads key=null → enters sync → sets dummyKey=K, dummyPassword=P → key=K, password=P → exits. Thread B: reads key=null (before A finished) → blocks on sync → enters → dummyKey != null → key = dummyKey (K), password = dummyPassword (P). Correct. But the assignment order is `dummyKey = derive(...)` then `dummyPassword = probe`. A thread C that reads `dummyKey` non-null *before* the sync block (i.e. its local `key` non-null) while `dummyPassword` is still null → the outer `if (key == null || password == null)` catches it: password==null → enter sync. Inside sync: `if (dummyKey == null)` false → `key = dummyKey; password = dummyPassword;` — password could still be null?? No: if dummyKey is non-null, then in the writer we set dummyKey first, then dummyPassword. So a reader inside the sync block can see dummyKey != null but dummyPassword == null (writer hasn't reached that line yet)... but wait, the writer holds the same lock (`synchronized(this)`) while doing both assignments. So any reader inside `synchronized(this)` cannot observe an interleaving. And a reader outside the sync block that captured password==null goes into the sync block. So it's safe. But then after the sync block, `password` is guaranteed non-null. OK. Then `derive(password, ...)` with non-null. Safe. No finding. \n\nHmm, but there is one path: thread C's local capture happens *before* acquiring the lock: `byte[] key = dummyKey; char[] password = dummyPassword;` If key != null && password != null → skip sync entirely. Since writer sets dummyKey first and both are volatile, can a reader see dummyKey=K (non-null) and dummyPassword=null? Yes — reader outside sync could read dummyKey=K (writer assigned it) and dummyPassword=null (writer hasn't assigned yet). Then the outer if triggers because password == null → goes to sync. Fine. Reverse: dummyKey non-null but password non-null while key... impossible to see dummyPassword non-null before dummyKey non-null (program order + volatile). So no null deref. Good.\n\nHowever — deadlock/perf: `synchronized(this)` around a KDF at 310k iterations (~600ms) means concurrent unknown-user logins serialize on first call only. Minor. Note: **on MySQL with the default collation (`utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci`) and on SQL Server with default collation, `VARCHAR` comparison with `=` is CASE-INSENSITIVE and trailing-space-insensitive, and the PRIMARY KEY is likewise case-insensitive.** So the code's documented behaviour (\"`Alice` would be rejected as a different account\") silently does NOT hold on MySQL/SQL Server — which README explicitly claims to support (\"accepted as written by H2, PostgreSQL, MySQL/MariaDB and SQL Server\"). On MySQL, `WHERE username = 'Alice'` matches row `alice`, so a user registering `Alice` could... well, the PK would reject registering `Alice` if `alice` exists (duplicate), so no account takeover; but login as `alice` with password of `Alice`... they're the same row anyway. The real risk: case-insensitive matching makes `authenticate(\"ALICE\")` succeed with alice's password, i.e. usernames become case-insensitive — usually acceptable. exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities\n36:# Exploiting SQL Injection Vulnerabilities\n38:## When to Use\n48:## Prerequisites\n56:## Workflow\n58:### Step 1: Injection Point Discovery\n69:### Step 2: Database Fingerprinting\n82:### Step 3: Manual Exploitation Techniques\n107:### Step 4: Automated Exploitation with sqlmap\n120:### Step 5: Impact Demonstration and Reporting\n130:## Key Concepts\n132:| Term | Definition |\n134:| **SQL Injection** | A code injection technique that exploits unvalidated user input in SQL queries to manipulate database operations, extract data, or execute administrative operations |\n135:| **Union-Based SQLi** | Injection technique that appends a UNION SELECT statement to the original query to extract data from other tables in the same response |\n136:| **Blind SQL Injection** | Injection where the application does not return query results directly; the attacker infers data through boolean responses or time delays |\n137:| **Parameterized Query** | A prepared SQL statement where user input is passed as parameters rather than concatenated into the query string, preventing injection |\n138:| **Second-Order Injection** | SQL injection where the malicious payload is stored by the application and executed in a different context or SQL query at a later time |\n139:| **Stacked Queries** | Executing multiple SQL statements separated by semicolons in a single request, enabling INSERT, UPDATE, or DELETE operations through injection |\n140:| **WAF Bypass** | Techniques for evading Web Application Firewall rules that block common SQL injection patterns, using encoding, alternate syntax, or fragmentation |\n142:## Tools & Systems\n149:## Common Scenarios\n151:### Scenario: SQL Injection in Healthcare Patient Portal\n170:## Output Alternative: document-and-rename. Since the README/javadoc already defers to the caller (`:175`), the honest fix: either take ownership (document + fill in finally) or don't retain `dummyPassword`. I'll present the ownership version plus the dummyPassword fix:\n```java\n    private void burnKeyDerivation() {\n        ...\n        byte[] throwawaySalt = new byte[saltBytes];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(throwawaySalt);\n        byte[] probe = derive(password, throwawaySalt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n        if (MessageDigest.isEqual(probe, key)) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"internal error: dummy credential collision\");\n        }\n    }\n```\nInstead of retaining the secret, retain only its length profile: re-derive a fresh random password each time (cost is the derivation itself anyway), or keep the password in a `char[]` that is cleared once `dummyKey` exists and rebuild it lazily when needed. Simplest: since `burnKeyDerivation` must spend KDF time with *some* password, generate a local random password per call and clear it immediately:\n```java\n        char[] secret = randomPassword();\n        byte[] throwawaySalt = new byte[saltBytes];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(throwawaySalt);\n        try {\n            byte[] probe = derive(secret, throwawaySalt, iterations, algorithm, hashBytes);\n            if (MessageDigest.isEqual(probe, dummyKey)) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\"internal error: dummy credential collision\");\n            }\n        } finally {\n            Arrays.fill(secret, '\\0');\n        }\n```\nand drop the `dummyPassword` field (the collision check then compares against `dummyKey` derived from a *different* salt, so it can still never match).
